\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\mathcal{M}^{\text{TT}}_{4}+\mathcal{M}_{4}^{L}.\end{split}

(107)

Note that only a single diagram contributes to the s-channel in the shadow formalism (in contrast to the SK or wavefunction-based formalisms). The other channels are then obtained by permuting the external legs and the full correlator is obtained by summing over all three channels.

Using the Feynman rules in the previous section, the transverse-tracless part is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{\text{TT}}&=\frac{1}{4}(\vec{k}_{1}^{i}\vec{k}_{2}^{j}+\vec{k}_{1}^{j}\vec{k}_{2}^{i})(\vec{k}_{3}^{n}\vec{k}_{4}^{m}+\vec{k}_{3}^{m}\vec{k}_{4}^{n})\Pi_{ijnm}\\
\times&\int dp\int_{0}^{\infty}dz^{\prime}dz\;(zz^{\prime})^{-1/2}(1+k_{1}z)(1+k_{2}z)e^{-k_{12}z}\frac{pJ_{-\frac{3}{2}}(pz)J_{-\frac{3}{2}}(pz^{\prime})}{p^{2}+y^{2}}(1+k_{3}z^{\prime})(1+k_{4}z^{\prime})e^{-k_{34}z^{\prime}}\end{split}

(108)

To obtain this expression, we only needed the TT part of the graviton propagator in (
93
) and we neglected the first term in (
104
) since
\delta_{ij}\Pi_{ijnm}=0
. Similarly, using the full interaction vertex in (
104
) and the longitudinal part of the propagator in (
93
), we obtain the following expression for the longitudinal part:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{L}&=\frac{1}{4}\int dp\int_{0}^{\infty}dzdz^{\prime}(zz^{\prime})^{-1/2}(1+k_{1}z)(1+k_{2}z)e^{-k_{12}z}pJ_{\frac{3}{2}}(pz)J_{\frac{3}{2}}(pz^{\prime})(1+k_{3}z^{\prime})(1+k_{4}z^{\prime})e^{-k_{34}z^{\prime}}\\
\times\Bigg[&\frac{1}{p^{2}y^{2}}\bigg(V^{12}_{ij}V^{34}_{nm}\big(G^{TL}_{ijnm}+\frac{y_{i}y_{j}y_{n}y_{m}}{y^{2}}\big)-2y^{2}\big(\vec{k}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{2}W_{34}+\vec{k}_{3}\cdot\vec{k}_{4}W_{12}\big)-3W_{12}W_{34}\bigg)\\
+&\frac{1}{p^{4}}\bigg(V^{12}_{ij}V^{34}_{nm}\frac{y_{i}y_{j}y_{n}y_{m}}{y^{2}}+y_{i}y_{j}\big(\vec{k}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{2}W_{34}+\vec{k}_{3}\cdot\vec{k}_{4}W_{12}\big)+y^{2}W_{12}W_{34}\bigg)\Bigg].\end{split}

(109)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{ij}^{12}&=(\vec{k}_{1}^{i}\vec{k}_{2}^{j}+\vec{k}_{1}^{j}\vec{k}_{2}^{i});\\
V_{nm}^{34}&=(\vec{k}_{3}^{n}\vec{k}_{4}^{m}+\vec{k}_{3}^{m}\vec{k}_{4}^{n}).\end{split}

(110)

After performing the integrals above
2
2

2

Note that the
z
integrals in the transverse-traceless part are finite in dimensional regulation. Additionally, the
p
integral contour is defined to avoid the pole at
p=0
. See appendix A of
36
for details.
we find that the transverse-traceless part is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{TT}=\tilde{f}_{22}y^{4}\Pi_{22}\end{split}

(111)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{f}_{22}&=f_{22}+\frac{g_{3}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{y})g_{3}(\vec{k}_{3},\vec{k}_{4},\vec{y})}{y^{3}}\\
f_{22}&=\frac{1}{E}-\frac{y^{2}}{EE_{L}E_{R}}+\frac{yk_{1}k_{2}}{EE_{L}^{2}E_{R}}+\frac{yk_{3}k_{4}}{EE_{R}^{2}E_{L}}+\frac{2yk_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}{E^{2}E_{L}^{2}E_{R}^{2}}-\frac{y(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4})}{E^{2}E_{L}E_{R}}\\
&\quad+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}}{E^{2}E_{L}}+\frac{k_{3}k_{4}}{E^{2}E_{R}}+\frac{2k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}{E^{3}E_{L}E_{R}},\\
g_{3}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{3})&=\frac{2k_{1}y^{2}+2k_{2}y^{2}+2k_{1}^{2}y+2k_{2}^{2}y+2k_{1}k_{2}y+k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+2k_{1}k_{2}^{2}+2k_{1}^{2}k_{2}+y^{3}}{\left(k_{1}+k_{2}+y\right){}^{2}}\\
\Pi_{22}&=\frac{24}{y^{2}}\Pi^{ijlm}k_{i}^{1}k_{j}^{2}k_{l}^{3}k_{m}^{4};\\
E&=k_{12}+k_{34},\qquad E_{L}=k_{12}+y,\qquad E_{R}=k_{34}+y,\qquad y=|\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}|.\end{split}

(112)

Moreover the longitudinal part can be expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{L}=f_{21}k^{2}\Pi_{21}+f_{20}(E_{L}E_{R}-kE)\Pi_{20}+f_{c}\end{split}

(113)

where

\displaystyle f_{21}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}{E^{3}}-\frac{k_{3}k_{4}k_{12}+k_{1}k_{2}k_{34}}{E^{2}}-\frac{k_{12}k_{34}}{E}=-f_{20},

\displaystyle\Pi_{21}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right)\left(k_{3}-k_{4}\right)\left(k^{2}\left(t^{2}-u^{2}\right)+\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right)\left(k_{3}-k_{4}\right)k_{12}k_{34}\right)}{k^{6}},

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\vec{k}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{4};\quad u=\vec{k}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{3};

\displaystyle\Pi_{20}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(1-\frac{3\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right){}^{2}}{y^{2}}\right)\left(1-\frac{3\left(k_{3}-k_{4}\right){}^{2}}{y^{2}}\right),

(114)

\displaystyle f_{c}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}\left(y^{2}+k_{12}k_{34}\right)}{E^{3}}-\frac{9E^{3}}{8}-\frac{3\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right){}^{2}\left(k_{3}-k_{4}\right){}^{2}\left(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)}{4Ey^{2}}

\displaystyle-3\left(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{4}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{2}k_{4}\right),

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{4}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{2}k_{4}\right)\left(y^{2}+k_{12}k_{34}\right)}{E^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8}E\Bigg(-\frac{6\left(k_{1}-k_{2}\right){}^{2}\left(k_{3}-k_{4}\right){}^{2}}{y^{2}}+3\left(2y^{2}+k_{12}^{2}+k_{34}^{2}\right)+34\left(k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}+k_{2}k_{4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+21\left(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)\Bigg)-\frac{1}{4E}\Bigg(8\left(k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}+k_{2}k_{4}\right){}^{2}+24k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(4\left(k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}+k_{2}k_{4}\right)+3\left(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)\right)\left(2y^{2}+k_{12}^{2}+k_{34}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)\left(9\left(k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3}+k_{1}k_{4}+k_{2}k_{4}\right)-12\left(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4}\right)\right)\Bigg)

The answer matches with known computations in the literature

4.4
1-loop example

Next let us the consider the 1-loop corrections to the scalar self-energy. As in scalar QED, we will only consider tadpole diagrams for simplicity.

These are depicted in Figure
5
.

k,-
k,-
\vec{l},-

+

k,-
k,-
\vec{l},+

Figure 5
:
The two contributions to the tadpole correlator from the shadow formalism in gravity.

Using the Feynman rules in (
104
) and (
105
) we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi(\vec{k})\phi(-\vec{k})}_{\rm tadpole}&=\int d^{3}l\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;2\mathcal{V}_{4,ijnm}(z)\frac{(1+kz)^{2}e^{-2kz}}{z}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\bigg[\frac{\Pi_{ijnm}}{p^{2}+l^{2}}\bigg(J_{-\frac{3}{2}}(pz)^{2}+J_{\frac{3}{2}}(pz)^{2}\bigg)+2J_{\frac{3}{2}}(pz)^{2}\tilde{T}_{ijnm}\bigg]\\
&=\int d^{3}l\Bigg(10+\frac{80k^{3}}{l^{3}}+\frac{56k}{l}-\frac{3l^{2}}{k^{2}}-\frac{16(6k^{5}+5k^{3}l^{2}-3k^{2}l^{3}-l^{5})(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{l})^{2}}{k^{4}l^{5}}\Bigg)\end{split}

(115)

Note that integral requires regularisation but we will not consider that in this paper.

Let us now verify that we obtain the same result using the wavefunction based approach. As explained in Appendix
B
, the are two wavefunction coefficients which contribute:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi(\vec{k})\phi(-\vec{k})}_{\rm tadpole}=\int d^{3}l\;\left(\psi_{2}^{(1)}(k,k,\vec{l})+\frac{\psi_{4}^{(0)}(k,k,l,l)}{2\psi_{2}^{(0)}(l)}\right)\end{split}

(116)

where
\psi_{2}^{(0)}(l)=-\frac{l^{3}}{2}

74
;
70
and the relative factor of
\frac{1}{2}
is a symmetry factor. The corresponding wavefunction coefficients are given by the following Feynman diagrams:

(117)

which correspond to the integral expressions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{2}^{(1)}&=\int d^{3}l\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,\mathcal{V}_{4,ijnm}(z)\frac{(1+kz)^{2}e^{-2kz}}{z}pJ_{\frac{3}{2}}(pz)^{2}\bigg[\frac{\Pi_{ijnm}}{p^{2}+l^{2}}+\tilde{T}_{ijnm}\bigg]\\
\psi_{4}^{(0)}&=\int d^{3}l\;\mathcal{V}_{4,ijnm}(z)\Pi_{ijnm}\frac{(1+kz)^{2}(1+lz)^{2}}{z^{4}}e^{-2kz}e^{-2lz}\end{split}

(118)

where the
\Pi_{ijnm}
in
\psi^{(0)}_{4}
comes from a polarisation sum. By evaluating the
p
and
z
integrals, we find perfect agreement with the result from the shadow action in (
115
).

4.5
Dressing rules and double copy

In section
3.3
we showed that the tree-level 4-point correlator in scalar QED can be obtained from flat space Feynman diagrams by applying certain dressing rules for the transverse and longitudinal parts of the photon propagator. In this section we will derive similar dressing rules for the correlator describing scalars exchanging a graviton. Since the longitudinal part of the exchange is very complicated and does not seem to be as canonical as the transverse part, we will focus our attention on the latter. It is conceivable that the longitudinal part can be reconstructed from the transverse part, as we discuss in the conclusion. Using the transverse dressing rules, we then show that the transverse part of the graviton exchange correlator can be obtained from that of gluon exchange in scalar QCD via a double copy inherited from flat space.

Let us now consider the s-channel contribution to the transverse part of the graviton exchange correlator in (
108
) and (
111
). With a bit of algebra we find that it can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{TT}&=\frac{V_{12}^{ij}V_{34}^{nm}}{4}\int dp\hat{\Delta}^{T}_{h}(k_{1},k_{2},p)\frac{\Pi_{ijnm}}{p^{2}+y^{2}}\hat{\Delta}^{T}_{h}(k_{3},k_{4},p)\\

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.82394pt}{-22.52013pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(119)

where the auxiliary propagators are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}_{h}^{T}(k_{i},k_{j},p)&=pk_{i}^{2}k_{j}^{2}\hat{\partial}_{p}\hat{\partial}_{k_{i}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,s\frac{k_{ij}+s}{(s+k_{ij})^{2}+p^{2}}\\
&=pk_{i}^{2}k_{j}^{2}\hat{\partial}_{p}\hat{\partial}_{k_{i}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{j}}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,s\hat{\Delta}_{\gamma}^{T}(k_{ij}+s,p)\end{split}

(120)

where
\hat{\Delta}_{\gamma}^{T}(k,p)
is the transverse gluon dressing given in (
65
) and
\hat{\partial}_{x}f\equiv\partial_{x}\left(\frac{f}{x}\right)
. We have used the notation
k_{ij}=k_{i}+k_{j}
. Hence, we can obtain the transverse part of the s-channel contribution to the graviton exchange correlator by simply taking the corresponding Feynman diagram in flat space, applying an auxiliary propagator to each vertex (which carries the non-conserved energy at each vertex in de Sitter space) and integrating over the energy flowing through the propagators. We can then obtain the transverse part of the full correlator by summing over all channels (where the other channels are obtained by permuting the external legs).

Let us now explain how to obtain this from the double copy procedure. First note that the 3-point vertices in (
119
) can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{12}^{ij}&=k_{1}^{i}k_{2}^{j}+k_{1}^{j}k_{2}^{i}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(y^{i}y^{j}-\alpha^{i}\alpha^{j}\right)\end{split}

(121)

where
\vec{y}=\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}
,
\vec{\alpha}=\vec{k}_{1}-\vec{k}_{2}
, and there is a similar expression for the right-hand vertex. Since
y^{i}\Pi_{ijnm}=0
, we can therefore write (
119
) as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4}^{TT}=\frac{\alpha^{i}\alpha^{j}\beta^{n}\beta^{m}\Pi_{ijnm}}{16}\int dp\hat{\Delta}_{h}^{T}\frac{1}{p^{2}+y^{2}}\hat{\Delta}_{h}^{T}\end{split}

(122)

where
\vec{\beta}=\vec{k}_{3}-\vec{k}_{4}
. Comparing this to the dressed gluon exchange diagram in (
65
), we see that the graviton diagram can be obtained by squaring the flat space numerators of the gluonic one and replacing gluonic auxiliary propagators with gravitational ones (recall that Feynman diagrams of scalar QED can be uplifted to those of scalar QCD ). Note that when squaring the numerator, we naively obtain
\alpha^{i}\alpha^{j}\beta^{n}\beta^{m}\left(\pi_{in}\pi_{jm}+\pi_{jn}\pi_{im}\right)
. We then subtract
\pi_{ij}\pi_{mn}
from the tensor structure in order to reproduce the transverse part of the graviton propagator. In flat space, this additional step does not effect the final result since it cancels out after summing over all three channels (see appendix
D
for more details). On the other hand, in de Sitter space this subtraction is required in order to obtain the correct result for the transverse part of the graviton exchange correlator. It would be interesting to understand the origin of this additional step more systematically as subtracting the contribution of an unwanted dilaton which arises from squaring a gluon.

5
Spinning correlators

In this section we will look at correlators with spinning external legs. First, we write 3-point gluon and graviton correlators in terms of dressed flat space Feynman diagrams and comment on their double copy structure. Then we recast the 4-point tree-level correlator in Yang-Mills in terms of dressed flat space Feynman diagrams and show that the numerators enjoy a kinematic Jacobi relation analogous to the one found for scalar QCD in (
75
).

5.1
Three points

Let us begin with the color-ordered 3-point gluon correlator
71
;
47
:

\left\langle JJJ\right\rangle=\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{3}\cdot p_{1}+{\rm cyclic}\right)k_{123}^{-1}

(123)

where the external polarisations point along the boundary, since we are working in axial gauge, and satisfy
\epsilon_{i}\cdot\vec{k}_{i}=0
. Note that the prefactor of the energy pole is precisely the 3-point scattering amplitude in flat space. Moreover, the energy pole arises from the dressing rule in (
66
) specialised to the case where all three legs are external (so
p=0
). We may therefore write the 3-point correlator simply as

(124)

where the diagram is a flat space Feynman diagram dressed with an auxiliary propagator. In the next subsection, we will see that the same dressing rule plays a role at higher points.

Next, let’s consider the 3-point graviton correlator
71
;
47
:

\left\langle TTT\right\rangle=\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{3}\cdot p_{1}+{\rm cyclic}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}{k_{123}^{2}}+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}+{\rm cyclic}}{k_{123}}-k_{123}\right)

(125)

This was obtained in
71
by taking the real part of the 3-point wavefunction. As shown in that reference, the latter exhibits an infrared divergence which cancels out after taking the real part (see section
2
for a review of how to relate wavefunction coefficients to in-in correlators). Moreover, the tensor structure in (
126
) is the three-point Einstein gravity amplitude in flat space, which is simply the square of the 3-point Yang-Mills amplitude. Hence, the double copy for 3-point correlator tensor structure is directly inherited from flat space, as first pointed out in
47
. This property can be made more manifest by writing the 3-point graviton correlator in terms of a dressed flat space Feynman diagram as follows:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.17914pt}{3.89098pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\left(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{3}\cdot p_{1}+{\rm cyclic}\right)^{2}\Delta_{hhh}(k_{123}).\end{split}

(126)

The second diagram is a flat space Feynman diagram dressed with the auxiliary propagator

\Delta_{hhh}(k_{123})=-k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}k_{3}^{2}\hat{\partial}_{k_{1}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{2}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{3}}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\frac{s}{s+k_{123}}\Bigg)

(127)

It is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{hhh}(k_{123})=\frac{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}{k_{123}^{2}}+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}+{\rm cyclic}}{k_{123}}-k_{123}.\end{split}

(128)

Note that the integral is divergent but becomes finite after acting with the derivatives. This dressing rule can also be realised as the leading part of the
p\to 0
limit of the dressing rule in (
120
) with an additional external leg:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta_{hhh}(k_{123})=\lim_{p\to 0}p\bigg(p(k_{1}k_{2}k_{3})^{2}\hat{\partial}_{p}\hat{\partial}_{k_{1}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{2}}\hat{\partial}_{k_{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dss\frac{k_{123}+s}{(s+k_{123})^{2}+p^{2}}\bigg).\end{split}

(129)

In summary, we can obtain the the 3-point graviton correlator from the 3-point gluon correlator by squaring the flat space amplitude in the dressed Feynman diagram and then replacing the gluonic dressing rule with a gravitational one. For other perspectives on the double copy of 3-point correlators, see
61
;
67
;
15
. In the next section, we will look at 4-point correlators in Yang-Mills theory from the perspective of dressing. We save an analysis of higher-point graviton correlators from this perspective for future work.

5.2
Four points

Now let us consider the color-ordered tree-level 4-point Yang-Mills correlator. Using the shadow formalism, the s-channel contribution is given by a sum of two diagrams:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{JJJJ}^{(s)}=\hbox to83.31pt{\vbox to100.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.6564pt\lower-50.20659pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 2.95 1.04 5.99 0.7 7.04 -1.13 C 8.1 -2.96 6.87 -5.75 4.5 -7.79 C 2.12 -9.82 -0.34 -10.49 -0.82 -9.66 C -1.3 -8.83 0.51 -7.03 3.46 -5.99 C 6.41 -4.95 9.44 -5.29 10.5 -7.12 C 11.56 -8.95 10.33 -11.75 7.96 -13.78 C 5.58 -15.82 3.12 -16.48 2.64 -15.65 C 2.16 -14.82 3.97 -13.02 6.92 -11.98 C 9.87 -10.95 12.9 -11.28 13.96 -13.11 C 15.02 -14.94 13.79 -17.74 11.42 -19.77 C 9.04 -21.81 6.58 -22.47 6.1 -21.64 C 5.62 -20.81 7.43 -19.01 10.38 -17.97 C 13.33 -16.94 16.36 -17.27 17.42 -19.1 C 18.48 -20.93 17.25 -23.73 14.87 -25.76 C 12.5 -27.8 10.04 -28.46 9.56 -27.63 C 9.08 -26.8 10.89 -25 13.84 -23.97 C 16.79 -22.93 19.82 -23.26 20.88 -25.09 C 21.93 -26.92 20.71 -29.72 18.33 -31.76 C 15.96 -33.79 13.5 -34.45 13.02 -33.62 C 12.54 -32.79 14.35 -30.99 17.3 -29.96 C 20.24 -28.92 23.28 -29.26 24.34 -31.09 C 25.39 -32.91 24.17 -35.71 21.79 -37.75 C 19.42 -39.78 16.96 -40.45 16.48 -39.62 C 16 -38.78 17.81 -36.99 20.76 -35.95 C 23.7 -34.91 26.74 -35.25 27.8 -37.08 C 28.85 -38.91 27.62 -41.7 25.25 -43.74 L 28.54 -49.44}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-34.00832pt}{40.42914pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{22.8972pt}{40.42914pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{22.8972pt}{-44.92914pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(130)

The analogous diagrams contributing to the color-ordered wavefunction were evaluated in
12
using Feynman rules derived in
79
. To adapt them to the shadow formalism, we simply split the gluon propagator in the exchange diagram into it transverse and longitudinal components and then flip the sign of the spectral parameter appearing the Bessel functions of the transverse part, which flips the boundary conditions of that component from Dirichlet to Neumann. In the end we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{JJJJ}^{(s)}&=V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\pi_{ij}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}e^{-k_{12z_{1}}}e^{-k_{34}z_{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dpp\frac{J_{-1/2}(pz_{1})J_{-1/2}(pz_{2})}{p^{2}+y^{2}}\\
&+V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{y^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}e^{-k_{12z_{1}}}e^{-k_{34}z_{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dpp\frac{J_{1/2}(pz_{1})J_{1/2}(pz_{2})}{p^{2}}\\
&+V_{c}^{(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dze^{-k_{1234}z}\end{split}

(131)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{ab}^{i}&=\epsilon_{a}\cdot\epsilon_{b}(\vec{k}_{a}-\vec{k}_{b})^{i}+2(\epsilon_{a}\cdot\vec{k}_{b})\epsilon_{b}^{i}-2(\epsilon_{b}\cdot\vec{k}_{a})\epsilon_{a}^{i}~.\\
V_{c}^{(s)}&=(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{3})(\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{4})-(\epsilon_{1}\cdot\epsilon_{4})(\epsilon_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{3}).\end{split}

(132)

The first line corresponds to the transverse part of the exchange, the second to the longitudinal part, and the third encodes a contact interaction. The
z
integrals arise from Wick-rotating dS
4
to EAdS
4
.

After performing the
z
integrals, we can write the correlator in terms of dressed flat space diagram, as depicted in Figure
6
.

1
2
3
4

+
1
2
3
4

Figure 6
:
Dressed diagrams contributing to the four-point Yang-Mills correlator. The dressing of the exchange diagram encodes both the transverse and longitudinal dressing rules.

In more detail, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{JJJJ}^{(s)}&=V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\pi_{ij}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\Delta_{\gamma}^{T}(k_{12},p)\Delta_{\gamma}^{T}(k_{34},p)\frac{1}{p^{2}+y^{2}}\\
&+V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{y^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\Delta_{\gamma}^{L}(k_{12},p)\Delta_{\gamma}^{L}(k_{34},p)\frac{1}{p^{2}}\\
&+V_{c}^{(s)}\Delta_{\gamma}^{T}(k_{1234},0)\end{split}

(133)

where
\Delta_{\gamma}^{T}
and
\Delta_{\gamma}^{L}
are the same transverse and longitudinal dressings that appeared in scalar QED. Note that
\Delta_{\gamma}^{T}
also dresses the 4-point contact interaction and the 3-point contact interaction in (
124
) and in those cases it reduces to an energy pole. While the transverse dressing rules can be applied to generic diagrams, the longitudinal dressing rule
\Delta_{\gamma}^{L}
is only valid for this diagram and would have to be reworked for higher points using the methods described in appendix E of
36
. Finally, note that we can recover the scalar QED result in (
56
) via generalised dimensional reduction
25
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\epsilon_{a}\cdot\epsilon_{b}\to 1,\qquad\epsilon_{a}\cdot k_{b}\to 0.\end{split}

(134)

It would be interesting to extend this to 4-point correlators of fermions
34
, external gravitons, which we leave for future work.

CK duality

In section
3.5
we showed that the kinematic numerators of the tree-level 4-point correlator of scalar QCD enjoy a certain kinematic Jacobi relation which reflects an underlying duality between color and kinematics. Let us now show that that this extends to the 4-point Yang-Mills correlator. Starting with (
133
), we may write the s-channel contribution to the color-ordered correlator as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{JJJJ}_{(s)}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{(p^{2}+k_{12}^{2})(p^{2}+k_{34}^{2})}\Bigg[k_{12}k_{34}\frac{V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\pi_{ij}}{p^{2}+y^{2}}+\frac{V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}y_{i}y_{j}}{y^{2}}+k_{12}k_{34}V_{c}^{(s)}\Bigg].\end{split}

(135)

As in the scalar QCD case, we pull out the Feynman propagator
(p^{2}+y^{2})^{-1}
and define the kinematic numerator to be the remaining part of the integrand:

\displaystyle\begin{split}n_{s}(p)=\frac{1}{(p^{2}+k_{12}^{2})(p^{2}+k_{34}^{2})}\Bigg[k_{12}k_{34}V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}\pi_{ij}+(p^{2}+y^{2})\frac{V_{12}^{i}V_{34}^{j}k_{i}k_{j}}{y^{2}}+(p^{2}+y^{2})k_{12}k_{34}V_{c}^{(s)}\Bigg]\end{split}

(136)

The analogous numerators in the
t
and
u
channels are then obtained by permuting the external labels according to (
72
). While the the numerators in all three channels do not directly add to zero as they do in flat space, they do so after integrating over
p
:

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dp\left(n_{s}(p)+n_{t}(p)+n_{u}(p)\right)=0.

(137)

The interested reader can find the full calculation explicitly verified at
49
. This relation also holds for the kinematic numerators of the wavefunction, similar to the case of scalar QCD described earlier.

We emphasize that for this choice of numerators the Jacobi relation is automatically satisfied without requiring energy conservation and one does not need to introduce the generalized gauge transformations
13
. This was previously seen for calculations of the correlator in Mellin space
4
;
77
and we now notice it as a novel feature for the correlator in momentum space.

6
Conclusion

In this paper we revisited the SK formalism for photons, gluons, and gravitons coupled to scalar fields in dS
4
and derived local actions in EAdS
4
(which we collectively refer to as the shadow formalism) whose Witten diagrams compute in-in correlators of these theories. We described how the residual gauge fixing at the boundary affected the propagators for the longitudinal modes in a non-trivial manner and verified the validity of this approach for a number of examples at tree-level and 1-loop. This also demonstrated its relative simplicity compared to more traditional approaches based on the SK and wavefunction-based formalisms. We then derived a set of dressing rules for uplifting flat space Feynman diagrams to photon and gluon exchange correlators and used them to propose an analogue of colour/kinematics duality for such correlators. Similarly, we found that the transverse traceless part of graviton exchange correlators can also be obtained by dressing flat space Feynman diagrams and derived from gluon exchange diagrams via a simple double copy prescription inherited from flat space.

There are numerous directions for further exploration. Firstly, it would be interesting to extend the dressing rules and double copy to the tree-level 4-point graviton in-in correlator. This is straightforward to do for the transverse traceless part of graviton exchange diagrams but reconstructing the longitudinal part in this way could be challenging. On the other hand, the longitudinal part is identical to that of the 4-graviton wavefunction which has been previously computed in
18
;
12
using bootstrap methods and the double copy. This leads to the broader question of whether the shadow formalism can be combined in a useful way with bootstrap methods such as the cosmological optical theorem
51
;
78
, manifestly local test
62
, soft limits
59
;
37
;
76
, and conformal Ward identities
21
;
22
;
13
;
45
to compute in-in correlators more efficiently. Another important direction would be to develop a more systematic understanding of loop-level in-in correlators in the framework of the shadow formalism. Indeed, in this paper we have only considered simple examples that do not receive contributions from ghosts and we did not attempt to regulate or renormalise them. For recent work on this direction, see for example
38
;
24
;
60
. Recently, the shadow action of a general higher-derivative scalar effective field theory in dS
4
was reconstructed from a boundary perspective by imposing crossing symmetry and certain anomalous dimension constraints on its in-in correlators
40
. It would be of great interest to use the results of this paper to generalise this analysis to gravitational effective field theories in dS
4
and deduce bounds on their coefficients analogous to those in AdS
28
. It would also be interesting to extend this formalism to compute correlation functions of gauge invariant operators such as Wilson loops and the field strength tensor
44
.
Finally, it would be be interesting to adapt the formalism developed in this paper to compute other physical observables including out-of-time-order correlators in de Sitter space
53
;
52
;
33
;
69
. We hope to address these questions in the future.

Appendix A
SK propagators

We review the derivation of the SK propagators following the approach of
94
. We describe the consequence of fixing the residual gauge invariance at the turning point and demonstrate how it leads to propagators satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Matching conditions

Consider a single scalar field
\varphi(\eta,\vec{x})
whose boundary value at
\eta=0
is denoted by
\phi(\vec{x})
. In our conventions
-\infty<\eta<0
. The correlators are computed at
\eta=0
and the boundary conditions at
\eta\to-\infty(1-i\epsilon)
are the standard Hartle-Hawking boundary conditions which implies
\varphi(-\infty(1-i\epsilon))=0
for
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. We evaluate the correlator
\braket{Q(\phi)}=\braket{\Psi|Q(\phi)|\Psi}
where
Q(\phi)
is some function of the fields
\phi
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}=\int D\phi D\phi^{\prime}\Psi(\phi)\Psi^{*}(\phi^{\prime})\delta(\phi-\phi^{\prime})Q(\phi)\end{split}

(138)

The wavefunction and its conjugate admit the following path integral representations,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi(\phi)&=\int_{0}^{\varphi^{>}(0)=\phi}D\varphi^{>}e^{iS[\varphi^{>}]},\qquad\Psi^{*}(\phi^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{\varphi^{<}(0)=\phi^{\prime}}D\varphi^{<}e^{-iS[\varphi^{<}]}~,\end{split}

(139)

where the dummy variables
\varphi^{>},\varphi^{<}
are meant to represent fields on the time-ordered and anti-time-ordered contour, respectively. We also use a shorthand notation
0
instead of
\varphi^{>/<}_{(-\infty(1-i\epsilon))}=0
for denoting the lower limit of the path integral. Combining (
138
) and (
139
) we get the following path integral representation for
\braket{Q(\phi)}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}=\int D\phi D\phi^{\prime}\delta(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\varphi^{>}(0)=\phi}D\varphi^{>}\int_{0}^{\varphi^{<}(0)=\phi^{\prime}}D\varphi^{<}\ Q(\phi)e^{iS[\varphi^{>}]}e^{-iS[\varphi^{<}]}\end{split}

(140)

At this stage it is convenient to spatially discretize the path integral and express the fields
\varphi(\eta,\vec{x})
in terms of discrete mode functions
\varphi(\eta,\vec{x})\rightarrow\varphi_{n}(\eta)
.
This step is not strictly essential but helps in making some steps more apparent. Hence in these notations the path integral over the field
D\varphi
is denoted as

\displaystyle\begin{split}D\varphi(\eta,\vec{x})\equiv\prod_{n}d\varphi_{n}(\eta)\end{split}

(141)

In this notation the path integrals over the field at
\eta=0
, i.e,
\int D\phi D\phi^{\prime}\delta(\phi-\phi^{\prime})
simply becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int D\phi D\phi^{\prime}\delta(\phi-\phi^{\prime})=\int\prod_{n}d\varphi^{>}_{n}(0)d\varphi^{<}_{n}(0)\prod_{m}\delta(\varphi_{m}^{>}(0)-\varphi_{m}^{<}(0))\end{split}

(142)

Combining these we get the familiar SK representation for (
140
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}=\int\prod_{n}d\varphi_{n}^{>}(\eta)d\varphi_{n}^{<}(\eta)Q(\varphi^{>}(0))\prod_{m}\delta(\varphi^{>}_{m}(0)-\varphi^{<}_{m}(0))e^{iS[\varphi^{>}]}e^{-iS[\varphi^{<}]}\end{split}

(143)

Without the matching condition of the fields at the boundary
\eta=0
the two path integrals would be completely decoupled. It is convenient to absorb the matching condition into the exponential via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prod_{m}\delta(\varphi_{m}^{>}(0)-\varphi_{m}^{<}(0))&=e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{m}(\varphi_{m}^{>}(0)-\varphi_{m}^{<}(0))^{2}}\\
&=e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int d\eta d\eta^{\prime}\sum_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta(\eta)\delta(\eta^{\prime})(\varphi_{m}^{>}(\eta)-\varphi_{m}^{<}(\eta))(\varphi_{m^{\prime}}^{>}(\eta^{\prime})-\varphi_{m^{\prime}}^{<}(\eta^{\prime}))}\\
&\equiv e^{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int d\eta d\eta^{\prime}\sum_{mm^{\prime}}C_{mm^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})(\varphi_{m}^{>}(\eta)-\varphi_{m}^{<}(\eta))(\varphi_{m^{\prime}}^{>}(\eta^{\prime})-\varphi_{m^{\prime}}^{<}(\eta^{\prime}))},\end{split}

(144)

where in the second step we have introduced some extra integrals to mimic the structure obtained from the standard quadratic action. The normalization of the delta function is unimportant for our discussion. The function
C_{mm^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
is given as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{mm^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta(\eta)\delta(\eta^{\prime})\end{split}

(145)

The actions
S[\varphi^{>}]
and
S[\varphi^{<}]
for a scalar field of mass
M
can also be expressed in the discretized form as

\displaystyle\begin{split}S[\varphi^{>}]&=\int^{0}_{-\infty}d\eta\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\alpha}\varphi^{>})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}M^{2}(\varphi^{>})^{2}\equiv\sum_{nn^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}d\eta d\eta^{\prime}D^{>}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\varphi^{>}_{n}(\eta)\varphi^{>}_{n^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime}),\\
S[\varphi^{<}]&=-\int^{0}_{-\infty}d\eta\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{\alpha}\varphi^{<})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}M^{2}(\varphi^{<})^{2}\equiv\sum_{nn^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}d\eta d\eta^{\prime}D^{<}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\varphi^{<}_{n}(\eta)\varphi^{<}_{n^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime}),\\
\end{split}

(146)

where
D^{>}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
D^{<}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
are the discretized version of the standard quadratic operator and are defined with an implicit
\delta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})
function in order to resemble (
145
). Hence up to an overall normalization,
\braket{Q(\phi)}
becomes

\displaystyle\braket{Q(\phi)}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{n}d\varphi_{n}^{>}d\varphi_{n}^{<}Q(\phi)

(147)

\displaystyle\times e^{\sum_{nn^{\prime}}\int d\eta\Big[iD^{>}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\varphi_{n}^{>}(\eta)\varphi_{n^{\prime}}^{>}(\eta^{\prime})-iD^{<}_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\varphi_{n}^{<}(\eta)\varphi_{n^{\prime}}^{<}(\eta^{\prime})-\frac{1}{\epsilon}C_{nn^{\prime}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})(\varphi_{n}^{>}(\eta)-\varphi_{n}^{<}(\eta))(\varphi_{n^{\prime}}^{>}(\eta^{\prime})-\varphi_{n^{\prime}}^{<}(\eta^{\prime}))\Big]}

The term inside the square bracket can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big[\cdots\Big]=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{>}&\varphi^{<}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}iD^{>}-\epsilon^{-1}C&\epsilon^{-1}C\\
\epsilon^{-1}C&-iD^{<}-\epsilon^{-1}C\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{>}\\
\varphi^{<}\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

(148)

where we have suppressed the
n,n^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}
dependences. The propagators for this system are obtained by inverting the matrix above:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}iD^{>}-\epsilon^{-1}C&\epsilon^{-1}C\\
\epsilon^{-1}C&-iD^{<}-\epsilon^{-1}C\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-iG^{>>}&iG^{><}\\
iG^{<>}&iG^{<<}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(149)

From this equation it is clear that by setting the matching condition
C=0
, we would not get a mixed propagator. By solving the equation above we obtain the following conditions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}D^{>}G^{>>}-\frac{i}{\epsilon}C(G^{>>}+G^{<>})&=1,\\
-D^{>}G^{><}+\frac{i}{\epsilon}C(G^{<<}-G^{><})&=0\\
D^{<}G^{<>}-\frac{i}{\epsilon}C(G^{>>}+G^{<>})&=0\\
D^{<}G^{<<}+\frac{i}{\epsilon}C(G^{><}-G^{<<})&=1\end{split}

(150)

Demanding that the equations are true for arbitrarily small
\epsilon
, we obtain the following constraints:

\displaystyle D^{>}G^{>>}

\displaystyle=D^{<}G^{<<}=1,

(151)

\displaystyle CG^{<<}

\displaystyle=CG^{><},\ CG^{>>}=-CG^{<>},

(152)

\displaystyle D^{>}G^{><}

\displaystyle=D^{<}G^{<>}=0

(153)

The solutions for the functions,
G^{>>}
and
G^{<<}
are given by the inverting (
151
) and are the standard time-ordered and anti-time-ordered differential propagators

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{nn^{\prime}}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})&=\braket{\mathcal{T}\phi_{n}(\eta)\phi_{n^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime})},\qquad G_{nn^{\prime}}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\braket{\bar{\mathcal{T}}\phi_{n}(\eta)\phi_{n^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime})},\end{split}

(154)

where
\mathcal{T}
and
\bar{\mathcal{T}}
refer to time-ordering and anti-time-ordering, respectively. The mixed propagators satisfy the homogeneous differential equations (
153
) with the initial condition set by (
152
) leading to the following solutions (for
\eta,\eta^{\prime}<0
, as that is the turning point of the contour),

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{nn^{\prime}}^{><}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})&=\braket{\phi_{n}(\eta)\phi_{n^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime})},\qquad G_{nn^{\prime}}^{<>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\braket{\phi_{n}(\eta^{\prime})\phi_{n^{\prime}}(\eta)}.\end{split}

(155)

These propagators resemble the usual SK propagators and demonstrates how the matching condition of the fields leads to the mixed propagators. We give explicit forms of the propagators for scalar fields and the transverse part of gauge fields below.

Scalar and transverse propagators

The position space propagators for the conformally coupled and the massless fields can be represented in this form,

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\nu}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{3/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{pH_{\nu}^{(1)}(p\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}-y^{2}+i\epsilon},\end{split}

(156)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\quad G_{\nu}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{3/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{pH_{\nu}^{(2)}(p\eta)H_{\nu}^{(1)}(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}-y^{2}-i\epsilon}\end{split}

(157)

where
\nu=\frac{1}{2}
(
\frac{3}{2}
) for conformally coupled (massless) fields. We perform each of these integrals carefully. First consider
G_{\nu}^{>>}(\eta)
. For
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\eta>\eta^{\prime}
the integral is analytic in the upper-half plane whereas for
\eta<\eta^{\prime}
it is analytic in the lower-half plane. Hence we can perform the integral by picking up residues. For this we note that the roots of the denominator are given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}p=y+i\epsilon,\qquad p=-y-i\epsilon\end{split}

(158)

The first case contributes while closing the contour from above and the second case while closing the contour from below. Evaluating the integral via residues then gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\nu}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{3/2}\frac{i}{2\pi}\Bigg[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{pH_{\nu}^{(1)}(p\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}-y^{2}+i\epsilon}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\;+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{pH_{\nu}^{(1)}(p\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}-y^{2}+i\epsilon}\Bigg]\\
&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{3/2}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})H_{\nu}^{(2)}(y\eta)H_{\nu}^{(1)}(y\eta^{\prime})+\Theta(-\eta)H_{\nu}^{(1)}(y\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(y\eta^{\prime})\Big].\end{split}

(159)

Similarly, for
G_{<<}(\eta)
we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\nu}^{<<}(\eta)=(\eta\eta^{\prime})^{3/2}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})H_{\nu}^{(1)}(y\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(y\eta^{\prime})+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)H_{\nu}^{(2)}(y\eta)H_{\nu}^{(1)}(y\eta^{\prime})\Big].\end{split}

(160)

For the purpose of finding the propagators for conformally coupled fields, which we couple to gauge fields, we set
\nu=\frac{1}{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\nu}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\frac{\eta\eta^{\prime}}{2y}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})e^{-iy(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)e^{iy(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}\Big],\end{split}

(161)

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\nu}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\frac{\eta\eta^{\prime}}{2y}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})e^{iy(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)e^{-iy(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}\Big],\end{split}

(162)

which are the standard expressions for the time-ordered and the anti-time-ordered propagators.

The mixed propagators
G^{><}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
G^{<>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
can be read from the coefficients of the Heaviside Theta functions,

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{><}(t,t^{\prime})&=\frac{\eta\eta^{\prime}}{2y}e^{iy(\eta-\eta^{\prime})},\\
G^{<>}(t,t^{\prime})&=\frac{\eta\eta^{\prime}}{2y}e^{iy(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)}.\end{split}

(163)

A similar form of the propagators is obtained for the transverse part of gauge fields with the tensor structure
\pi_{ij}
(when evaluated in flat space) is shown in (
32
).

Decoupling condition

We now discuss the implication of residual gauge fixing at the turning point of the SK path integral. This is applicable for the longitudinal component of the gauge field as discussed in section
3.1
. To avoid a clutter of indices this can be understood by considering two scalar fields
a,\phi
such that
a
vanishes at the boundary. The
a
field mimics the longitudinal component of the gauge field. Thus the only correlators that survive on the boundary are of the field
\phi
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}=\int D\phi DaQ(\phi)\delta(a)|\Psi(\phi,a)|^{2}\end{split}

(164)

where we have denoted the vanishing of the field
a
at the boundary with the insertion of the
\delta(a)
function. This plays the role of the residual gauge fixing condition as described in section
3
. The wavefunction
\Psi(\phi,a)
and its complex conjugate
\Psi^{*}(\phi,a)
are now given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi(\phi,a)&=\int_{0}^{\varphi_{>}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{>}\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{>}(0)=a}D\tilde{a}_{>}e^{iS[\varphi_{>},\tilde{a}_{>}]},\\
\Psi^{*}(\phi,a)&=\int_{0}^{\varphi_{<}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{<}\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{<}(0)=a}D\tilde{a}_{<}e^{-iS[\varphi_{<},\tilde{a}_{<}]}~.\end{split}

(165)

Plugging these integral representations into (
164
) we get,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}&=\int D\phi Da\delta(a)\int_{0}^{\varphi_{>}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{>}\int_{0}^{\varphi_{<}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{<}\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{<}(0)=a}D\tilde{a}_{<}\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{>}(0)=a}D\tilde{a}_{>}\\
&\qquad\times e^{iS[\varphi_{>},\tilde{a}_{>}]}e^{-iS[\varphi_{<},\tilde{a}_{<}]}Q(\phi).\end{split}

(166)

Performing the integral over
a
on the support of the
\delta(a)
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{Q(\phi)}&=\int D\phi\int_{0}^{\varphi_{>}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{>}\int_{0}^{\varphi_{<}(0)=\phi}D\varphi_{<}Q(\phi)\\
&\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{>}(0)=0}D\tilde{a}_{>}e^{iS[\varphi_{>},\tilde{a}_{>}]}\int_{0}^{\tilde{a}_{<}(0)=0}D\tilde{a}_{<}e^{-iS[\varphi_{<},\tilde{a}_{<}]}\end{split}

(167)

Therefore the integrals over
a_{>}
and
a_{<}
are decoupled and produce no mixed propagator (as the analog of the
C_{mn}
matrix (
145
) is absent here). Since the field
a
vanishes at the boundary its Green function satisfies the Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Longitudinal propagators

For a field satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions, the momentum space propagators are given by replacing the Hankel Functions in (
156
) and (
157
) with Bessel Functions
79
which our case are of the form
\sin(p\eta)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{D}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=(\eta\eta^{\prime})i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{\sin(p\eta)\sin(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}+i\epsilon},\qquad G_{D}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=-(\eta\eta^{\prime})i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp}{2\pi}\frac{\sin(p\eta)\sin(p\eta^{\prime})}{p^{2}-i\epsilon}\end{split}

(168)

Note the similarity between these equations and the Green functions for the longitudinal mode given in (
33
). The extra factors of
\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{y^{2}}
will not be important for the analysis below and hence we strip them off. Explicit computation gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{D}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}(\eta\eta^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\big[\eta^{\prime}-(-1)^{1/4}\eta\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\epsilon}\big]+(\eta\eta^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\big[\eta-(-1)^{1/4}\eta\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\epsilon}\big]\\
&=(\eta\eta^{\prime})\frac{i}{2}\Big[\Theta(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\eta^{\prime}+\Theta(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)\eta\Big]=-G_{D}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}).\end{split}

(169)

Since the
a
fields are decoupled at the boundary as explained in
A
the mixed propagators are zero:

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{D}^{><}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=G_{D}^{<>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=0~.\end{split}

(170)

From this equation and (
168
) it is easy to see that these propagators satisfy the unitarity condition of the Green function
64
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{D}^{>>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})+G_{D}^{<<}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})+G_{D}^{><}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})+G_{D}^{<>}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=0.\end{split}

(171)

Appendix B
Correlators from wavefunctions

In this appendix we review the derivation of the in-in correlators from wavefunction coefficients and derive some of the formulae used in the body of the paper. We remind the reader that we drop overall factors of
k^{-\rho}
with
\rho=1
for conformally coupled scalars/gluons and
\rho=3
for massless scalars/gravitons, in the final formulae (see (
177
) for an example). For concreteness, we will mainly focus on scalar QED and briefly comment on the analogous computations in gravity coupled to massless scalars, which are very similar.

Let us consider correlators of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\Psi|\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2})\cdots\phi(\vec{k}_{2n-1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2n})|\Psi}\\
&\qquad\qquad=\int D\phi D\phi^{\dagger}D\vec{A}_{T}|\Psi(\vec{A}_{T},\phi,\phi^{\dagger})|^{2}\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2})\cdots\phi(\vec{k}_{2n-1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2n})\end{split}

(172)

where we have set the normalization of the wavefunction to 1. The wavefunction
\Psi(\vec{A}_{T},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)
has the following expansion:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi(\vec{A}_{T},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)&=e^{-\int\psi^{ij}_{2}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{j}(-\vec{k})d^{3}k}e^{-\int\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k})\phi^{\dagger}(-\vec{k})\phi(\vec{k})d^{3}k}\\
&\times\exp\Bigg[\int\psi_{3i}^{A_{T}\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{3})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})d^{3}k_{1}d^{3}k_{2}d^{3}k_{3}\delta(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}+\vec{k}_{3})+\cdots\Bigg],\end{split}

(173)

where
\cdots
denotes the other wavefunction coefficients.
The quadratic wavefunction coefficients outside the square bracket are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi^{ij}_{2}(\vec{k},-\vec{k})=\pi^{ij}k,\qquad\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k},-\vec{k})=k.\end{split}

(174)

Tree-level 4-point

Let us consider the following 4-point function in scalar QED:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\phi_{4}}=\int D\phi^{\dagger}D\phi D\vec{A}_{T}|\Psi(\vec{A}_{T},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)|^{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\phi_{4}\end{split}

(175)

where we have suppressed the momentum dependence. From the expansion (
173
) we can deduce the expansion of the square of the wavefunction
|\Psi(\vec{A},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)|^{2}
, which can be effectively treated as an action for the path integral whose coefficients are given by the real parts of the wavefunction coefficients. In the present case
\text{Re}\psi_{4}=\psi_{4}
(see
93
for a discussion on more general cases).To compute the correlator to
O(g^{2})
, we need the following terms in the expansion of
|\Psi|^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\Psi(\vec{A}_{T},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)|^{2}&=e^{-\int\psi^{ij}_{2}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{j}(-\vec{k})d^{3}k}e^{-\int\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k})\phi^{\dagger}(-\vec{k})\phi(\vec{k})d^{3}k}\\
&\quad\times\Bigg[\int\psi_{4}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{4})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})d^{3}k_{1}\cdots d^{3}k_{4}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\int\psi_{3i}^{A_{T}\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2},\vec{k}_{3})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})d^{3}k_{1}d^{3}k_{2}d^{3}k_{3}\Big)^{2}\Bigg],\end{split}

(176)

where we have suppressed the momentum conserving delta function in each term.
Using Wick contractions, we then find the following two contributions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi_{2}\phi^{\dagger}_{3}\phi_{4}}=\frac{1}{\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{4})}\Bigg[\psi_{4}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi\phi^{\dagger}\phi}+\psi_{3i}^{A_{T}\phi^{\dagger}\phi}\psi_{3j}^{A_{T}\phi^{\dagger}\phi}\frac{\pi^{ij}}{k}\Bigg].\end{split}

(177)

The expressions for the wavefunctions are given in (
49
) and (
50
). In practice, we will ignore the prefactor consisting of two-point functions.

The analogous computation in gravity is very similar. In particular the formula for the 4-point scalar correlator is similar to (
177
) but with more indices:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{4}}=\psi_{4}^{\phi\phi\phi\phi}+\psi_{3ij}^{h_{TT}\phi\phi}\psi_{3mn}^{h_{TT}\phi\phi}\frac{\Pi^{ijmn}}{y^{3}}\end{split}

(178)

where the factor
\frac{\Pi^{ijmn}}{y^{3}}
arises from the polarization sums of the transverse-traceless gravitons.

1-loop tadpole

Next let’s derive the 1-loop tadpole contribution to the scalar 2-point function in (
62
). The following terms in (
173
) will be needed for this purpose:

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\Psi(\vec{A},\phi^{\dagger},\phi)|^{2}&=e^{-\int\psi^{ij}_{2}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k})A_{T}^{j}(-\vec{k})d^{3}k}e^{-\int\psi_{2}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k})\phi^{\dagger}(-\vec{k})\phi(\vec{k})d^{3}k}\\
&\quad\times\Bigg[\int\psi_{4ij}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi A_{T}A_{T}}(\vec{k}_{1},\cdots,\vec{k}_{4})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})A_{T}^{i}(\vec{k}_{3})A_{T}^{j}(\vec{k}_{4})d^{3}k_{1}\cdots d^{3}k_{4}\\
&\qquad+\int\psi_{2}^{(1)\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2})\phi^{\dagger}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})d^{3}k_{1}d^{3}k_{2}\Bigg].\end{split}

(179)

The two-point function can then be computed by performing Wick contractions. To
\mathcal{O}(g^{2})
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\phi^{\dagger}_{1}\phi_{2}}=\int d^{3}l\Bigg[\frac{\pi^{ij}}{2l}\psi_{4ij}^{\phi^{\dagger}\phi A_{T}A_{T}}(\vec{k},\vec{k},\vec{l},\vec{l})+\psi_{2}^{(1)\phi^{\dagger}\phi}(\vec{k},\vec{k})\Bigg],\end{split}

(180)

where we have ignored the overall factors depending on the 2-point functions of the external momenta. The expression for the wavefunction coefficients are computed in (
63
).

Appendix C
Graviton 2-point functions

In this Appendix, we will provide more details about the derivation of (
93
). Starting with the graviton decomposition
h_{ij}=h^{TT}_{ij}+\chi\delta_{ij}+\partial_{(i}\xi_{j)}
, with
\partial_{i}h^{TT}_{ij}=0
and
h^{TT}_{ii}=0
, the action (
84
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\text{free gr}}&=S_{\text{TT}}\Bigl[h^{TT}_{ij}\Bigr]+S_{\chi+\xi}\Bigl[\xi_{i},\chi\Bigr],\end{split}

(181)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\text{TT}}&=\int d^{4}x\eta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\eta}h^{TT}_{ij}\partial_{\eta}h^{TT}_{ij}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{k}h^{TT}_{ij}\partial_{k}h^{TT}_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-2}h^{TT}_{ij}h^{TT}_{ij}\Bigr),\end{split}

(182)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\chi+\xi}&=\int d^{4}x\eta^{2}\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\Bigl(-6\partial_{\eta}\chi\partial_{\eta}\chi-4\partial_{\eta}\chi\partial_{\eta}\partial\cdot\xi+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\eta}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}\partial_{\eta}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\eta}\partial\cdot\xi\partial_{\eta}\partial\cdot\xi\Bigr)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-2}\Bigl(-6\chi^{2}-4\chi\partial\cdot\xi+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\partial\cdot\xi\partial\cdot\xi\Bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\chi\partial_{i}\chi\bigg].\end{split}

(183)

Next we Wick rotate to EAdS
4
following (
12
). We will only consider the
>
branch of SK contour for simplicity. The action for this branch becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\chi+\xi}^{\text{ E}}&=\int d^{4}xz^{2}\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\Bigl(-6\partial_{z}\chi\partial_{z}\chi-4\partial_{z}\chi\partial_{z}\partial\cdot\xi+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}\partial_{z}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}\partial\cdot\xi\partial_{z}\partial\cdot\xi\Bigr)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}z^{-2}\Bigl(-6\chi^{2}-4\chi\partial\cdot\xi+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}\partial_{j}\xi_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\partial\cdot\xi\partial\cdot\xi\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\chi\partial_{i}\chi\bigg].\end{split}

(184)

The action can be further simplified if we decompose
\xi_{i}=\hat{\xi}_{i}+\partial_{i}\sigma
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\chi+\xi}^{\text{ E}}&=\int d^{4}xz^{2}\bigg[\frac{1}{4}\Bigl(-6\partial_{z}\chi\partial_{z}\chi-4\partial_{z}\chi\partial_{z}\partial^{2}\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}\partial_{j}\hat{\xi}_{i}\partial_{z}\partial_{j}\hat{\xi}_{i}\Bigr)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}z^{-2}\Bigl(-6\chi^{2}-4\chi\partial^{2}\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\hat{\xi}_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\xi}_{i}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\chi\partial_{i}\chi\biggr].\end{split}

(185)

Recalling that the longitudinal modes obey Dirichlet boundary conditions, we find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\chi^{>}(\vec{x},z)\chi^{>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{E}}&=0\\
\langle\chi^{>}(\vec{x},z)\sigma^{>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{E}}&=\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{\text{i}\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}\frac{J_{3/2}(pz)J_{3/2}(pz^{\prime})}{y^{2}p^{2}}\\
\langle\sigma^{>}(\vec{x},z)\sigma^{>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{E}}&=\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{\text{i}\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}\frac{J_{3/2}(pz)J_{3/2}(pz^{\prime})(y^{2}+3p^{2})}{y^{4}p^{4}}\\
\langle\hat{\xi}^{>}_{i}(\vec{x},z)\hat{\xi}^{>}_{j}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{\text{E}}&=\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{\text{i}\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}\frac{4J_{3/2}(pz)J_{3/2}(pz^{\prime})\pi_{ij}}{y^{2}p^{2}}.\end{split}

(186)

Recall that
h^{L}_{ij}=\chi\delta_{ij}+\partial_{(i}\xi_{j)}=\chi\delta_{ij}+\partial_{(i}\hat{\xi}_{j)}+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\sigma
. From this we deduce the following 2-point functions of longitudinal modes:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle h_{ij}^{L>}(\vec{x},z)h_{kl}^{L>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})\rangle_{E}\\
&=\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{\text{i}\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}J_{3/2}(pz)J_{3/2}(pz^{\prime})\bigg[\frac{1}{k^{2}p^{2}}\Bigl(\pi_{ik}y_{j}y_{l}+\pi_{il}y_{j}y_{k}+\pi_{jk}y_{i}y_{l}+\pi_{jl}y_{i}y_{k}\\
&-y_{i}y_{j}\pi_{kl}-y_{k}y_{l}\pi_{ij}\Bigr)+y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}y_{l}\frac{(y^{2}+p^{2})}{y^{4}p^{4}}\bigg].\end{split}

(187)

The 2-point functions of
h_{ij}^{L<}
take a similar form and
\braket{h^{L>}h^{L<}}_{E}=\braket{h^{L<}h^{L>}}_{E}=0
. Moreover, the 2-point functions of the transverse-traceless modes of the graviton are straightforward to derive and are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{h_{ij}^{TT>}(\vec{x},z)h_{mn}^{TT>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})}_{E}&=\Pi_{ijmn}\frac{(zz^{\prime})^{1/2}}{2}\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}\frac{H_{3/2}^{(2)}(pz)H_{3/2}^{(1)}(pz^{\prime})}{p^{2}+y^{2}},\\
\braket{h_{ij}^{TT<}(\vec{x},z)h_{mn}^{TT<}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})}_{E}&=\Pi_{ijmn}\frac{(zz^{\prime})^{1/2}}{2}\int\frac{pdpd^{3}y}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})}\frac{H_{3/2}^{(1)}(pz)H_{3/2}^{(2)}(pz^{\prime})}{p^{2}+y^{2}},\\
\braket{h_{ij}^{TT>}(\vec{x},z)h_{mn}^{TT<}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})}_{E}&=0,\\
\braket{h_{ij}^{TT<}(\vec{x},z)h_{mn}^{TT>}(\vec{x}^{\prime},z^{\prime})}_{E}&=0.\end{split}

(188)

To obtain the 2-point functions of the shadow fields, we perform the following field redefinitions which unmix the 2-point functions of the transverse-traceless modes:

\displaystyle\begin{split}h^{-}_{ij}&=-\frac{h^{>}_{ij}+h^{<}_{ij}}{2},\qquad h^{+}_{ij}=-i\frac{h^{>}_{ij}-h^{<}_{ij}}{2}.\end{split}

(189)

After implementing this change of basis, we obtain the two point functions given in (
93
).

Appendix D
Flat space double copy

In this Appendix, we will review the double copy relating tree-level 4-point scattering amplitudes of massless scalars exchanging gluons and gravitons in flat space. Sine the amplitudes are gauge-invariant we will work in de Donder gauge for simplicity. Recall that in de Donder gauge (
g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}=0
where
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\rho}
is the Christoffel symbol) the graviton propagator is

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}=\frac{\eta_{\mu\rho}\eta_{\nu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}\eta_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\sigma\rho}}{p^{2}}\equiv\frac{T_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}}{p^{2}}\end{split}

(190)

and the scalar-graviton three-point vertex is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hbox to60.6pt{\vbox to66.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.89726pt\lower-33.11598pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 1.97 0 C 2.71 0 3.08 2.76 3.81 2.76 C 4.35 2.76 4.81 1.41 5.29 0 C 5.77 -1.41 6.23 -2.76 6.77 -2.76 C 7.3 -2.76 7.76 -1.41 8.24 0 C 8.72 1.41 9.18 2.76 9.72 2.76 C 10.25 2.76 10.71 1.41 11.2 0 C 11.68 -1.41 12.14 -2.76 12.67 -2.76 C 13.21 -2.76 13.67 -1.41 14.15 0 C 14.63 1.41 15.09 2.76 15.62 2.76 C 16.16 2.76 16.62 1.41 17.1 0 C 17.58 -1.41 18.04 -2.76 18.58 -2.76 C 19.11 -2.76 19.57 -1.41 20.05 0 C 20.54 1.41 21 2.76 21.53 2.76 C 22.27 2.76 22.64 0 23.38 0 L 23.38 0 L 38.74 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 1.97 0 C 2.71 0 3.08 2.76 3.81 2.76 C 4.35 2.76 4.81 1.41 5.29 0 C 5.77 -1.41 6.23 -2.76 6.77 -2.76 C 7.3 -2.76 7.76 -1.41 8.24 0 C 8.72 1.41 9.18 2.76 9.72 2.76 C 10.25 2.76 10.71 1.41 11.2 0 C 11.68 -1.41 12.14 -2.76 12.67 -2.76 C 13.21 -2.76 13.67 -1.41 14.15 0 C 14.63 1.41 15.09 2.76 15.62 2.76 C 16.16 2.76 16.62 1.41 17.1 0 C 17.58 -1.41 18.04 -2.76 18.58 -2.76 C 19.11 -2.76 19.57 -1.41 20.05 0 C 20.54 1.41 21 2.76 21.53 2.76 C 22.27 2.76 22.64 0 23.38 0 L 23.38 0 L 38.74 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\bigg(p_{\mu}q_{\nu}+p_{\nu}q_{\mu}-\eta_{\mu\nu}p^{\lambda}q_{\lambda}\bigg)\equiv V^{qp}_{\mu\nu}.\end{split}

(191)

For the four-point exchange, we wish to write this in terms of the colour-ordered scalar QCD vertex factors and the exchanged momentum:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha^{\mu}&=(p_{1}-p_{2})^{\mu},\\
\beta^{\mu}&=(p_{3}-p_{4})^{\mu},\\
y^{\mu}&=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{\mu}=-(p_{3}+p_{4})^{\mu}.\end{split}

(192)

To do this, note that for four-momenta in flat space we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{1}^{\mu}k_{2}^{\nu}&=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}y_{\mu}y_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\alpha_{\mu}\alpha_{\nu},\\
\eta^{\mu\nu}k_{3}^{\mu}k_{4}^{\nu}&=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}y_{\mu}y_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\beta_{\mu}\beta_{\nu},\\
k_{1\mu}k_{2\nu}+k_{1\nu}k_{2\mu}&=\frac{1}{2}\big(y_{\mu}y_{\nu}-\alpha_{\mu}\alpha_{\nu}\big).\end{split}

(193)

Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{split}V^{12}_{\mu\nu}&=\frac{\kappa}{4}\bigg(y_{\mu}y_{\nu}-\alpha_{\mu}\alpha_{\nu}+\eta_{\mu\nu}\alpha^{2}\bigg),\\
V^{34}_{\mu\nu}&=\frac{\kappa}{4}\bigg(y_{\mu}y_{\nu}-\beta_{\mu}\beta_{\nu}+\eta_{\mu\nu}\beta^{2}\bigg),\end{split}

(194)

and the s-channel contribution to the amplitude is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{4,\rm{GR}}^{(s)}=\hbox to100.77pt{\vbox to66.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.38338pt\lower-33.11598pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
